At work especially, I take longer on breaks because I’m checking it or trying to fix how I look, and then I come back late. My manager has noticed, and I’m also slower at the register sometimes. I’m worried it makes me seem unreliable, especially because I’m trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the spot on your nose and cheek only about how it looks, or are you also worried about other aspects of it, like pain or function?\n\nPatient: It’s really just about how it looks. I don’t have pain or anything like that. I just keep getting stuck on how that tiny uneven spot looks, especially in photos or under the store lights, and I feel like people notice it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the spot on your nose and cheek is proportionate to its actual appearance?\n\nPatient: Probably not, honestly. It’s a really tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and people tell me they don’t even see it. But in photos or under the store lights, I get stuck on it, and it feels like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions, like checking or fixing your appearance, over and over again because of this concern?\n\nPatient: Yes. On my breaks I keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror, and I’ll try to fix my hair so it kind of covers that area. I also catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I spend too long doing all that and end up late getting back to the register.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep getting really stuck on this small uneven spot around my nose and cheek. At work especially, I take longer on breaks because I’m checking it or trying to fix how I look, and then I come back late. My manager has noticed, and I’m also slower at the register sometimes. I’m worried it makes me seem unreliable, especially because I’m trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the spot on your nose and cheek only about how it looks, or are you also worried about other aspects of it, like pain or function?\n\nPatient: It’s really just about how it looks. I don’t have pain or anything like that. I just keep getting stuck on how that tiny uneven spot looks, especially in photos or under the store lights, and I feel like people notice it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the spot on your nose and cheek is proportionate to its actual appearance?\n\nPatient: Probably not, honestly. It’s a really tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and people tell me they don’t even see it. But in photos or under the store lights, I get stuck on it, and it feels like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions, like checking or fixing your appearance, over and over again because of this concern?\n\nPatient: Yes. On my breaks I keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror, and I’ll try to fix my hair so it kind of covers that area. I also catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I spend too long doing all that and end up late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: When you check or fix your appearance, do you feel less anxious about the spot on your nose and cheek afterwards?\n\nPatient: Only for a little bit, maybe. Like I’ll check it or fix my hair around it and feel okay for a minute, but then I start wondering if it still looks bad. So I’ll go back to the bathroom mirror on my break or compare myself to girls at the register, and sometimes I spend too long doing it and get back late.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to make yourself feel better or less anxious when you check or fix your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s what I’m trying to do. Like on my breaks I check my face in the bathroom mirror or fix my hair around that spot, and I compare myself to girls coming through my line. It’s supposed to make me feel better, but sometimes I just get stuck doing it and end up late back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check or fix your appearance, like it's something you can't avoid doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it feels like I kind of have to. Like even if I tell myself I’m just going to finish my break, I end up going to the bathroom mirror to check my face, or fixing my hair around that spot. Sometimes I take too long doing it and get back to the register late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions to check or fix your appearance are causing you a lot of stress or emotional pain?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does stress me out. It’s not like I want to keep doing it, but on my breaks I end up checking my face in the bathroom mirror, fixing my hair around it, comparing myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I take so long that I’m late getting back to the register, and then I feel worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop checking or fixing your appearance if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I can tell myself I’m not going to do it, but then on my break I still end up in the bathroom checking my face or fixing my hair around it. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep getting really stuck on this small uneven spot around my nose and cheek. I notice it a lot in photos and under the lights at work, and even when people tell me they don’t see anything, it still feels like it’s the first thing customers or my managers notice.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about the spot on your nose and cheek affecting your daily activities or interactions with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. At work especially, I take longer on breaks because I’m checking it or trying to fix how I look, and then I come back late. My manager has noticed, and I’m also slower at the register sometimes. I’m worried it makes me seem unreliable, especially because I’m trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the spot on your nose and cheek only about how it looks, or are you also worried about other aspects of it, like pain or function?\n\nPatient: It’s really just about how it looks. I don’t have pain or anything like that. I just keep getting stuck on how that tiny uneven spot looks, especially in photos or under the store lights, and I feel like people notice it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the spot on your nose and cheek is proportionate to its actual appearance?\n\nPatient: Probably not, honestly. It’s a really tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and people tell me they don’t even see it. But in photos or under the store lights, I get stuck on it, and it feels like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions, like checking or fixing your appearance, over and over again because of this concern?\n\nPatient: Yes. On my breaks I keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror, and I’ll try to fix my hair so it kind of covers that area. I also catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I spend too long doing all that and end up late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: When you check or fix your appearance, do you feel less anxious about the spot on your nose and cheek afterwards?\n\nPatient: Only for a little bit, maybe. Like I’ll check it or fix my hair around it and feel okay for a minute, but then I start wondering if it still looks bad. So I’ll go back to the bathroom mirror on my break or compare myself to girls at the register, and sometimes I spend too long doing it and get back late.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to make yourself feel better or less anxious when you check or fix your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s what I’m trying to do. Like on my breaks I check my face in the bathroom mirror or fix my hair around that spot, and I compare myself to girls coming through my line. It’s supposed to make me feel better, but sometimes I just get stuck doing it and end up late back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check or fix your appearance, like it's something you can't avoid doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it feels like I kind of have to. Like even if I tell myself I’m just going to finish my break, I end up going to the bathroom mirror to check my face, or fixing my hair around that spot. And at work I keep comparing myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I take too long doing it and get back to the register late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions to check or fix your appearance are causing you a lot of stress or emotional pain?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does stress me out. It’s not like I want to keep doing it, but on my breaks I end up checking my face in the bathroom mirror, fixing my hair around it, comparing myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I take so long that I’m late getting back to the register, and then I feel worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop checking or fixing your appearance if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I can tell myself I’m not going to do it, but then on my break I still end up in the bathroom checking my face or fixing my hair around it. And I compare myself to girls who come through my line too. Sometimes I get stuck on it long enough that I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your behavior related to the spot on your nose and cheek is interfering with your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s affecting work more than friends or family. My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late and moving slower at the register. I’m worried it makes me look unreliable, especially because I’m trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or fixing your appearance is something you're doing in response to an anxiety or fear, rather than just because you want to look good?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s more because I get anxious about it. Like on my breaks, I’ll end up checking my face in the bathroom mirror or fixing my hair around it, not really because I’m trying to look nice, but because I feel like I have to make sure it doesn’t look bad. And then I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I get stuck doing it so long I’m late getting back to the register.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep getting really stuck on this small uneven spot around my nose and cheek. I notice it a lot in photos and under the lights at work, and even when people tell me they don’t see anything, it still feels like it’s the first thing customers or my managers notice.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about the spot on your nose and cheek affecting your daily activities or interactions with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. At work especially, I take longer on breaks because I’m checking it or trying to fix how I look, and then I come back late. My manager has noticed, and I’m also slower at the register sometimes. I’m worried it makes me seem unreliable, especially because I’m trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the spot on your nose and cheek only about how it looks, or are you also worried about other aspects of it, like pain or function?\n\nPatient: It’s really just about how it looks. I don’t have pain or anything like that. I just keep getting stuck on how that tiny uneven spot looks, especially in photos or under the store lights, and I feel like people notice it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the spot on your nose and cheek is proportionate to its actual appearance?\n\nPatient: Probably not, honestly. It’s a really tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and people tell me they don’t even see it. But in photos or under the store lights, I get stuck on it, and it feels like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions, like checking or fixing your appearance, over and over again because of this concern?\n\nPatient: Yes. On my breaks I keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror, and I’ll try to fix my hair so it kind of covers that area. I also catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I spend too long doing all that and end up late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: When you check or fix your appearance, do you feel less anxious about the spot on your nose and cheek afterwards?\n\nPatient: Only for a little bit, maybe. Like I’ll check it or fix my hair around it and feel okay for a minute, but then I start wondering if it still looks bad. So I’ll go back to the bathroom mirror on my break or compare myself to girls at the register, and sometimes I spend too long doing it and get back late.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to make yourself feel better or less anxious when you check or fix your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s what I’m trying to do. Like on my breaks I check my face in the bathroom mirror or fix my hair around that spot, and I compare myself to girls coming through my line. It’s supposed to make me feel better, but sometimes I just get stuck doing it and end up late back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check or fix your appearance, like it's something you can't avoid doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it feels like I kind of have to. Like even if I tell myself I’m just going to finish my break, I end up going to the bathroom mirror to check my face, or fixing my hair around that spot. And at work I keep comparing myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I take too long doing it and get back to the register late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions to check or fix your appearance are causing you a lot of stress or emotional pain?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does stress me out. It’s not like I want to keep doing it, but on my breaks I end up checking my face in the bathroom mirror, fixing my hair around it, comparing myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I take so long that I’m late getting back to the register, and then I feel worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop checking or fixing your appearance if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I can tell myself I’m not going to do it, but then on my break I still end up in the bathroom checking my face or fixing my hair around it. And I compare myself to girls who come through my line too. Sometimes I get stuck on it long enough that I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your behavior related to the spot on your nose and cheek is interfering with your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s affecting work more than friends or family. My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late and moving slower at the register. I’m worried it makes me look unreliable, especially because I’m trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or fixing your appearance is something you're doing in response to an anxiety or fear, rather than just because you want to look good?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s more because I get anxious about it. Like on my breaks, I’ll end up checking my face in the bathroom mirror or fixing my hair around it, not really because I’m trying to look nice, but because I feel like I have to make sure it doesn’t look bad. And then I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I get stuck doing it so long I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you decide to check or fix your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not really. It feels like I decide not to, but then on my break I still end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror or fixing my hair around it. And even when I’m working, I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I get caught up in it and take too long, and then I’m late getting back to the register.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. And even when I’m working, I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I get caught up in it and take too long, and then I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your appearance are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. It’s not ruining everything, but it is affecting my day-to-day. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep getting really stuck on this small uneven spot around my nose and cheek. I notice it a lot in photos and under the lights at work, and even when people tell me they don’t see anything, it still feels like it’s the first thing customers or my managers notice.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about the spot on your nose and cheek affecting your daily activities or interactions with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. At work especially, I take longer on breaks because I’m checking it or trying to fix how I look, and then I come back late. My manager has noticed, and I’m also slower at the register sometimes. I’m worried it makes me seem unreliable, especially because I’m trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the spot on your nose and cheek only about how it looks, or are you also worried about other aspects of it, like pain or function?\n\nPatient: It’s really just about how it looks. I don’t have pain or anything like that. I just keep getting stuck on how that tiny uneven spot looks, especially in photos or under the store lights, and I feel like people notice it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the spot on your nose and cheek is proportionate to its actual appearance?\n\nPatient: Probably not, honestly. It’s a really tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and people tell me they don’t even see it. But in photos or under the store lights, I get stuck on it, and it feels like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions, like checking or fixing your appearance, over and over again because of this concern?\n\nPatient: Yes. On my breaks I keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror, and I’ll try to fix my hair so it kind of covers that area. I also catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I spend too long doing all that and end up late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: When you check or fix your appearance, do you feel less anxious about the spot on your nose and cheek afterwards?\n\nPatient: Only for a little bit, maybe. Like I’ll check it or fix my hair around it and feel okay for a minute, but then I start wondering if it still looks bad. So I’ll go back to the bathroom mirror on my break or compare myself to girls at the register, and sometimes I spend too long doing it and get back late.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to make yourself feel better or less anxious when you check or fix your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s what I’m trying to do. Like on my breaks I check my face in the bathroom mirror or fix my hair around that spot, and I compare myself to girls coming through my line. It’s supposed to make me feel better, but sometimes I just get stuck doing it and end up late back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check or fix your appearance, like it's something you can't avoid doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it feels like I kind of have to. Like even if I tell myself I’m just going to finish my break, I end up going to the bathroom mirror to check my face, or fixing my hair around that spot. And at work I keep comparing myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I take too long doing it and get back to the register late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions to check or fix your appearance are causing you a lot of stress or emotional pain?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does stress me out. It’s not like I want to keep doing it, but on my breaks I end up checking my face in the bathroom mirror, fixing my hair around it, comparing myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I take so long that I’m late getting back to the register, and then I feel worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop checking or fixing your appearance if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I can tell myself I’m not going to do it, but then on my break I still end up in the bathroom checking my face or fixing my hair around it. And I compare myself to girls who come through my line too. Sometimes I get stuck on it long enough that I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your behavior related to the spot on your nose and cheek is interfering with your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s affecting work more than friends or family. My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late and moving slower at the register. I’m worried it makes me look unreliable, especially because I’m trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or fixing your appearance is something you're doing in response to an anxiety or fear, rather than just because you want to look good?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s more because I get anxious about it. Like on my breaks, I’ll end up checking my face in the bathroom mirror or fixing my hair around it, not really because I’m trying to look nice, but because I feel like I have to make sure it doesn’t look bad. And then I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I get stuck doing it so long I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you decide to check or fix your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not really. It feels like I decide not to, but then on my break I still end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror or fixing my hair around it. And even when I’m working, I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I get caught up in it and take too long, and then I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your appearance are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. It’s not ruining everything, but it is affecting my day-to-day. My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late and moving slower at the register, and I’m worried it makes me look unreliable, especially since I’m trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking or fixing your appearance because you feel like you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I have to. Like, I’ll tell myself I’m just going on break, but then I end up checking my face in the bathroom mirror or fixing my hair around it. And even at the register, I catch myself comparing my face to girls coming through my line. Sometimes I spend too long on it and I’m late getting back.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

---------- turn 16 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep getting really stuck on this small uneven spot around my nose and cheek. I notice it a lot in photos and under the lights at work, and even when people tell me they don’t see anything, it still feels like it’s the first thing customers or my managers notice.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about the spot on your nose and cheek affecting your daily activities or interactions with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. At work especially, I take longer on breaks because I’m checking it or trying to fix how I look, and then I come back late. My manager has noticed, and I’m also slower at the register sometimes. I’m worried it makes me seem unreliable, especially because I’m trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the spot on your nose and cheek only about how it looks, or are you also worried about other aspects of it, like pain or function?\n\nPatient: It’s really just about how it looks. I don’t have pain or anything like that. I just keep getting stuck on how that tiny uneven spot looks, especially in photos or under the store lights, and I feel like people notice it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the spot on your nose and cheek is proportionate to its actual appearance?\n\nPatient: Probably not, honestly. It’s a really tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and people tell me they don’t even see it. But in photos or under the store lights, I get stuck on it, and it feels like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions, like checking or fixing your appearance, over and over again because of this concern?\n\nPatient: Yes. On my breaks I keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror, and I’ll try to fix my hair so it kind of covers that area. I also catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I spend too long doing all that and end up late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: When you check or fix your appearance, do you feel less anxious about the spot on your nose and cheek afterwards?\n\nPatient: Only for a little bit, maybe. Like I’ll check it or fix my hair around it and feel okay for a minute, but then I start wondering if it still looks bad. So I’ll go back to the bathroom mirror on my break or compare myself to girls at the register, and sometimes I spend too long doing it and get back late.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to make yourself feel better or less anxious when you check or fix your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s what I’m trying to do. Like on my breaks I check my face in the bathroom mirror or fix my hair around that spot, and I compare myself to girls coming through my line. It’s supposed to make me feel better, but sometimes I just get stuck doing it and end up late back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check or fix your appearance, like it's something you can't avoid doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it feels like I kind of have to. Like even if I tell myself I’m just going to finish my break, I end up going to the bathroom mirror to check my face, or fixing my hair around that spot. And at work I keep comparing myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I take too long doing it and get back to the register late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions to check or fix your appearance are causing you a lot of stress or emotional pain?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does stress me out. It’s not like I want to keep doing it, but on my breaks I end up checking my face in the bathroom mirror, fixing my hair around it, comparing myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I take so long that I’m late getting back to the register, and then I feel worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop checking or fixing your appearance if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I can tell myself I’m not going to do it, but then on my break I still end up in the bathroom checking my face or fixing my hair around it. And I compare myself to girls who come through my line too. Sometimes I get stuck on it long enough that I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your behavior related to the spot on your nose and cheek is interfering with your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s affecting work more than friends or family. My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late and moving slower at the register. I’m worried it makes me look unreliable, especially because I’m trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or fixing your appearance is something you're doing in response to an anxiety or fear, rather than just because you want to look good?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s more because I get anxious about it. Like on my breaks, I’ll end up checking my face in the bathroom mirror or fixing my hair around it, not really because I’m trying to look nice, but because I feel like I have to make sure it doesn’t look bad. And then I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I get stuck doing it so long I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you decide to check or fix your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not really. It feels like I decide not to, but then on my break I still end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror or fixing my hair around it. And even when I’m working, I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I get caught up in it and take too long, and then I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your appearance are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. It’s not ruining everything, but it is affecting my day-to-day. My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late and moving slower at the register, and I’m worried it makes me look unreliable, especially since I’m trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking or fixing your appearance because you feel like you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I have to. Like, I’ll tell myself I’m just going on break, but then I end up checking my face in the bathroom mirror or fixing my hair around it. And even at the register, I catch myself comparing my face to girls coming through my line. Sometimes I spend too long on it and I’m late getting back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop thinking about the spot on your nose and cheek if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I try to tell myself it’s just a tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, but then I see it in photos or under the store lights and I get stuck on it. Even when people say they don’t notice it, it still feels like it’s the first thing customers or managers see.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I don’t have pain or anything like that. I just keep getting stuck on how that tiny uneven spot looks, especially in photos or under the store lights, and I feel like people notice it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the spot on your nose and cheek is proportionate to its actual appearance?\n\nPatient: Probably not, honestly. It’s a really tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and people tell me they don’t even see it. But in photos or under the store lights, I get stuck on it, and it feels like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions, like checking or fixing your appearance, over and over again because of this concern?\n\nPatient: Yes. On my breaks I keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror, and I’ll try to fix my hair so it kind of covers that area. I also catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I spend too long doing all that and end up late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: When you check or fix your appearance, do you feel less anxious about the spot on your nose and cheek afterwards?\n\nPatient: Only for a little bit, maybe. Like I’ll check it or fix my hair around it and feel okay for a minute, but then I start wondering if it still looks bad. So I’ll go back to the bathroom mirror on my break or compare myself to girls at the register, and sometimes I spend too long doing it and get back late.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to make yourself feel better or less anxious when you check or fix your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s what I’m trying to do. Like on my breaks I check my face in the bathroom mirror or fix my hair around that spot, and I compare myself to girls coming through my line. It’s supposed to make me feel better, but sometimes I just get stuck doing it and end up late back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check or fix your appearance, like it's something you can't avoid doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it feels like I kind of have to. Like even if I tell myself I’m just going to finish my break, I end up going to the bathroom mirror to check my face, or fixing my hair around that spot. And at work I keep comparing myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I take too long doing it and get back to the register late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions to check or fix your appearance are causing you a lot of stress or emotional pain?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does stress me out. It’s not like I want to keep doing it, but on my breaks I end up checking my face in the bathroom mirror, fixing my hair around it, comparing myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I take so long that I’m late getting back to the register, and then I feel worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop checking or fixing your appearance if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I can tell myself I’m not going to do it, but then on my break I still end up in the bathroom checking my face or fixing my hair around it. And I compare myself to girls who come through my line too. Sometimes I get stuck on it long enough that I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your behavior related to the spot on your nose and cheek is interfering with your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s affecting work more than friends or family. My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late and moving slower at the register. I’m worried it makes me look unreliable, especially because I’m trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or fixing your appearance is something you're doing in response to an anxiety or fear, rather than just because you want to look good?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s more because I get anxious about it. Like on my breaks, I’ll end up checking my face in the bathroom mirror or fixing my hair around it, not really because I’m trying to look nice, but because I feel like I have to make sure it doesn’t look bad. And then I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I get stuck doing it so long I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you decide to check or fix your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not really. It feels like I decide not to, but then on my break I still end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror or fixing my hair around it. And even when I’m working, I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I get caught up in it and take too long, and then I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your appearance are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. It’s not ruining everything, but it is affecting my day-to-day. My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late and moving slower at the register, and I’m worried it makes me look unreliable, especially since I’m trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking or fixing your appearance because you feel like you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I have to. Like, I’ll tell myself I’m just going on break, but then I end up checking my face in the bathroom mirror or fixing my hair around it. And even at the register, I catch myself comparing my face to girls coming through my line. Sometimes I spend too long on it and I’m late getting back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop thinking about the spot on your nose and cheek if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I try to tell myself it’s just a tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, but then I see it in photos or under the store lights and I get stuck on it. Even when people say they don’t notice it, it still feels like it’s the first thing customers or managers see.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face because you're trying to escape a feeling of anxiety or unease?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Like when I start feeling uneasy about it, I’ll go to the bathroom on my break and check my face in the mirror, or fix my hair around it. At the register I’ll compare myself to girls coming through my line too. It’s like I’m trying to calm myself down, but then I take too long and end up late getting back.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I notice it a lot in photos and under the lights at work, and even when people tell me they don’t see anything, it still feels like it’s the first thing customers or my managers notice.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about the spot on your nose and cheek affecting your daily activities or interactions with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. At work especially, I take longer on breaks because I’m checking it or trying to fix how I look, and then I come back late. My manager has noticed, and I’m also slower at the register sometimes. I’m worried it makes me seem unreliable, especially because I’m trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the spot on your nose and cheek only about how it looks, or are you also worried about other aspects of it, like pain or function?\n\nPatient: It’s really just about how it looks. I don’t have pain or anything like that. I just keep getting stuck on how that tiny uneven spot looks, especially in photos or under the store lights, and I feel like people notice it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the spot on your nose and cheek is proportionate to its actual appearance?\n\nPatient: Probably not, honestly. It’s a really tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and people tell me they don’t even see it. But in photos or under the store lights, I get stuck on it, and it feels like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions, like checking or fixing your appearance, over and over again because of this concern?\n\nPatient: Yes. On my breaks I keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror, and I’ll try to fix my hair so it kind of covers that area. I also catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I spend too long doing all that and end up late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: When you check or fix your appearance, do you feel less anxious about the spot on your nose and cheek afterwards?\n\nPatient: Only for a little bit, maybe. Like I’ll check it or fix my hair around it and feel okay for a minute, but then I start wondering if it still looks bad. So I’ll go back to the bathroom mirror on my break or compare myself to girls at the register, and sometimes I spend too long doing it and get back late.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to make yourself feel better or less anxious when you check or fix your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s what I’m trying to do. Like on my breaks I check my face in the bathroom mirror or fix my hair around that spot, and I compare myself to girls coming through my line. It’s supposed to make me feel better, but sometimes I just get stuck doing it and end up late back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check or fix your appearance, like it's something you can't avoid doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it feels like I kind of have to. Like even if I tell myself I’m just going to finish my break, I end up going to the bathroom mirror to check my face, or fixing my hair around that spot. And at work I keep comparing myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I take too long doing it and get back to the register late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions to check or fix your appearance are causing you a lot of stress or emotional pain?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does stress me out. It’s not like I want to keep doing it, but on my breaks I end up checking my face in the bathroom mirror, fixing my hair around it, comparing myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I take so long that I’m late getting back to the register, and then I feel worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop checking or fixing your appearance if you want to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I can tell myself I’m not going to do it, but then on my break I still end up in the bathroom checking my face or fixing my hair around it. And I compare myself to girls who come through my line too. Sometimes I get stuck on it long enough that I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your behavior related to the spot on your nose and cheek is interfering with your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s affecting work more than friends or family. My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late and moving slower at the register. I’m worried it makes me look unreliable, especially because I’m trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or fixing your appearance is something you're doing in response to an anxiety or fear, rather than just because you want to look good?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s more because I get anxious about it. Like on my breaks, I’ll end up checking my face in the bathroom mirror or fixing my hair around it, not really because I’m trying to look nice, but because I feel like I have to make sure it doesn’t look bad. And then I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I get stuck doing it so long I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you decide to check or fix your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not really. It feels like I decide not to, but then on my break I still end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror or fixing my hair around it. And even when I’m working, I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I get caught up in it and take too long, and then I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your appearance are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. It’s not ruining everything, but it is affecting my day-to-day. My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late and moving slower at the register, and I’m worried it makes me look unreliable, especially since I’m trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking or fixing your appearance because you feel like you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I have to. Like, I’ll tell myself I’m just going on break, but then I end up checking my face in the bathroom mirror or fixing my hair around it. And even at the register, I catch myself comparing my face to girls coming through my line. Sometimes I spend too long on it and I’m late getting back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop thinking about the spot on your nose and cheek if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I try to tell myself it’s just a tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, but then I see it in photos or under the store lights and I get stuck on it. Even when people say they don’t notice it, it still feels like it’s the first thing customers or managers see.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face because you're trying to escape a feeling of anxiety or unease?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Like when I start feeling uneasy about it, I’ll go to the bathroom on my break and check my face in the mirror, or fix my hair around it. At the register I’ll compare myself to girls coming through my line too. It’s like I’m trying to calm myself down, but then I take too long and end up late getting back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance are significantly interfering with your ability to do your job or interact with others at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is starting to. My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late, and I think I’m moving slower at the register too. I’m worried it makes me look unreliable, especially because I’m trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no choice but to check or fix your appearance when you start thinking about the spot on your nose and cheek?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. Once I start thinking about it, it feels really hard not to check. On my breaks I end up going to the bathroom mirror, fixing my hair around it, or looking at girls who come through my line and comparing. Sometimes I spend too long doing that and I’m late getting back to the register.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I just keep getting stuck on how that tiny uneven spot looks, especially in photos or under the store lights, and I feel like people notice it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the spot on your nose and cheek is proportionate to its actual appearance?\n\nPatient: Probably not, honestly. It’s a really tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and people tell me they don’t even see it. But in photos or under the store lights, I get stuck on it, and it feels like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions, like checking or fixing your appearance, over and over again because of this concern?\n\nPatient: Yes. On my breaks I keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror, and I’ll try to fix my hair so it kind of covers that area. I also catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line.
